DATE           THURSDAY                           MARCH                               5, 2020

                                       Public Hearing

                THIS BEING THE TIME heretofore set for a public hearing to receive public
comment regarding an Ordinance amending Title 5: “Business Licenses and Regulations”, of the
White City Municipal Code of Ordinance by adding public nuisance, crime-magnet, and general
welfare justifications for revoking a business license and providing greater due process for
business license suspensions and revocations.

               Paul Ashton, Legal Counsel, White City Metro Township, reviewed the proposed
ordinance. He stated this ordinance gives the Council additional authority to revoke business
licenses based on nuisance, crime magnet, and general welfare principles. The ordinance also
spells out greater due process protections. It would be a useful tool for the Council to have.

               Council Member Perry, seconded by Council Member Price, moved to open the
public hearing. The motion passed unanimously.

               Renae Sutton stated after reading through the ordinance she would like the
Council to review the following:

    5.07.20 (A) “Licensee or permittee has been convicted of a felony or any crime involving
     moral turpitude.” She felt that this needed additional clarification.

    5.07.20 (C) “Licensee or permittee has failed to pay personal property taxes, Utah State
     sales taxes, or other required fees.” She would like clarification if this is one time or
     repetitive failure to pay taxes.

    5.07.20 (I) “Licensee or permittee has permitted its employees, agents, or patrons to
     engage in illegal activities on the business premises or to use the premises for any
     unlawful purpose or enterprise or the maintenance of a public or private nuisance, as
     defined by applicable law, regulation, or ordinance.” She requested that the word
     “knowingly” be added to this section.

             Mr. Ashton stated this ordinance mirrors what is being considered by other
townships. The proposed ordinance would make enforcement easier.

               Council Member Price, seconded by Council Member Dickerson, moved to close
the public hearing. The motion passed unanimously.

                Council Member Little reviewed the following changes he would like to propose
within the ordinance:

    Section 5.07.20 (A) “Licensee or permittee has been convicted of a felony or any crime
   involving moral turpitude.”

             Council Member Little stated it does not seem fair for the Council to be able to
go back 20+ years looking for a felony and then not grant a license because of something that



                                              2
DATE           THURSDAY                             MARCH                                  5, 2020

happened a long time ago. He suggested the ordinance be changed to state “If a licensee or
permittee commits a felony while license…” That would relate closer to what is going on.

             Council Member Little moved to amend the language to state “If a licensee or
permittee commits a felony while licensed.” The motion died due to the lack of a second.

               Council Member Little, seconded by Council Member Price, moved to change the
language to read “licensee or permittee commits a felony or any crime involving moral turpitude
after having received a license.” The motion passed unanimously.

    Section 5.07.20 (C) “Licensee or permittee has failed to pay personal property taxes, Utah
   State sales taxes, or other required fees.”

              Council Member Little asked if the Council really cared about the licensee or
permittee paying personal property taxes or state sales tax in order to be licensed.

              Mr. Ashton stated it is included as grounds to revoke a license.

              Mayor Flint stated it should be included as one of the reasons to revoke a license.

    Section 5.07.20 (I) “Licensee or permittee has permitted its employees, agents, or patrons
   to engage in illegal activities on the business premises or to use the premises for any unlawful
   purpose or enterprise or the maintenance of a public or private nuisance, as defined by
   applicable law, regulation, or ordinance.”

              Council Member Little, seconded by Council Member Price, moved to add the word
“knowingly” before permitted its employees, agents, or patrons to engage….” The motion passed
unanimously.

    Section 5.07.030 (D) - After conducting a hearing pursuant to this section, the council may
   suspend or revoke any license or permit if the licensee or permittee fails to show cause by a
   preponderance of the evidence why the license or permit should not be suspended or revoked.

                Mr. Ashton stated the difference between the proposed ordinance and this one is
that it puts the obligation of showing why a license should not be revoked on the licensee or
permittee. The burden has shifted.

              Council Member Little stated in fairness the Council should not be able to say it
wants to revoke a license and then make the licensee or permittee show proof as to why it should
not. The Council needs to show a reason why it wants to revoke the licensee not the other way
around.

                 Mr. Ashton stated the courts issue orders to show cause all the time. Then the
plaintiff has to show proof as to why they disagreed.

                Council Member Little moved to delete this paragraph. The motion failed due to
the lack of a second.


                                                3
DATE            THURSDAY                             MARCH                                  5, 2020


    Section 5.07.030 (E) - If the licensee or permittee fails, neglects, or refuses to appear at
   the hour and date set for such hearing, the council may proceed to determine the matter in
   the absence of the licensee or permittee, or may, in its sole discretion, continue the date of
   such hearing to some later date, and at the later date proceed to act on the matter without
   further notice to the licensee or permittee.

                 Council Member Little stated if a person fails, neglects, or refuses to appear then
the city has the right to continue the hearing to a later time without any notice to the licensee. He
felt that notice should be given and proposed the Council should end the paragraph after the word
“matter” and remove the part of the sentence that reads “without further notice of the licensee or
permittee.”

              Mr. Ashton stated he would agree, the Council could get rid of those words,
because the courts demand that due process be given.

               Council Member Little, seconded by Council Member Price, moved to remove
“without further notice of the licensee or permittee” ending the sentence after the word matter.
The motion passed unanimously. Council Member Dickerson was absent for the vote

    Section 7: Pursuant to Utah Code Ann. §§ 10-3-711(1)(b) & 10-3-712, this ordinance shall
   take effect twenty (20) days after: (a) depositing a copy of the ordinance in the White City
   Metro Township’s recorder office; and (b) publishing a short summary of the ordinance in a
   newspaper of general circulation within the metro township.

             Council Member Little asked who the Recorder’s Office is for the White City
Metro Township.

              Mr. Ashton stated the County Clerk is the official recorder for the White City Metro
Township as indicated in the contract.

               Council Member Perry, seconded by Council Member Price, moved to approve the
following Ordinance as amended.

ORDINANCE NO. 20-02-01                                                         Date: March 5, 2020

       AN ORDINANCE AMENDING TITLE 5, ENTITLED “BUSINESS LICENSES AND
       REGULATIONS,” OF THE WHITE CITY METRO TOWNSHIP CODE OF
       ORDINANCES, BY ADDING PUBLIC NUISANCE, CRIME-MAGNET, AND
       GENERAL WELFARE JUSTIFICATIONS FOR REVOKING A BUSINESS
       LICENSE AND PROVIDING GREATER DUE PROCESS FOR BUSINESS
       LICENSE SUPENSIONS AND REVOCATIONS

                                            RECITALS




                                                 4
DATE            THURSDAY                             MARCH                                 5, 2020

      WHEREAS, Utah Code Ann. §§ 10-1-203(2) and 10-8-84 authorize the White City Metro
Township Council (the “Council”) to regulate businesses within the Township through the
enactment of city ordinances; and

        WHEREAS, the Utah Supreme Court has recognized that a city’s municipal authority to
issue licenses and promulgate licensing policies is a principal “means of regulating businesses”
pursuant to the “exercise of its police powers” (Consolidation Coal Co. v. Emery County, 702 P.3d
121, 123 (1985)); and

       WHEREAS, the Utah Supreme Court has declared that a city “has broad discretion in
granting, denying, or revoking [business] licenses” (Whiting v. Clayton, 617 P.2d 364, 364 (1980));
and

       WHEREAS, Utah Code Ann. § 10-8-60 authorizes the Council to regulate public
nuisances by “declar[ing] what shall be a nuisance, . . . abat[ing] the same, and impos[ing] fines
upon persons who may create, continue or suffer nuisances to exist”; and

        WHEREAS, the Utah Supreme Court has affirmed that “cities have been granted the
specific authority to pass ordinances to regulate, by means of business licensing, offensive
businesses that have become nuisances” (Dairy Prod. Servs., Inc. v. City of Wellsville, 2000 UT
81, ¶ 38; 13 P.3d 581, 591 (2000)); and

       WHEREAS, the Utah Supreme Court has further declared that “a [business] license
revocation may be based on the existence of a nuisance as defined by [city] ordinance” (Whiting,
617 P.2d at 365); and

       WHEREAS, the Utah Supreme Court has stated that a business license represents “a
substantial property interest” and therefore “should not be destroyed nor disrupted arbitrarily, nor
without following fundamental standards of due process of law to guard against capricious or
oppressive administrative action” (Anderson v. Utah County Bd. of County Comm’rs, 589 P.2d
1214, 1216 (1979)); and

       WHEREAS, the Utah Supreme Court has noted that the “minimum requirements” for due
process in revoking a business license are “adequate notice and an opportunity to be heard in a
meaningful manner” (Dairy Prod. Servs., Inc., 2000 UT at ¶ 49; 13 P.3d at 593); and

       WHEREAS, the White City Metro Township Council desires to amend the business license
revocation provisions of the White City Municipal Code to provide for greater due process in
business license suspension and revocation proceedings and to authorize business license
revocation or suspension in situations where businesses that become public or private nuisances,
encourage and facilitate crime, or operate in a manner injurious or manifestly inimical to the
general welfare and prosperity of the community.

       NOW, THEREFORE, the Council ordains as follows:

       Section 1: Section 5.07.020, entitled “Grounds For License Suspension Or Revocation,”
is repealed in its entirety and replaced as follows:


                                                 5
DATE           THURSDAY                              MARCH                                  5, 2020


5.07.20        Grounds for License/Permit Suspension or Revocation

      In addition to any fine imposed, every license or permit issued by the license official or
      council may be revoked or suspended by the council for any of the following reasons:

          A. Licensee or permittee has been convicted of a felony or any crime involving moral
             turpitude;

          B. Licensee or permittee has obtained, or aided another to obtain, a license by fraud
             or deceit;

          C. Licensee or permittee has failed to pay personal property taxes, Utah state sales
             taxes, or other required fees;

          D. Licensee or permittee has refused to permit authorized officers or employees to
             make an inspection or to take a sample of a commodity, or has interfered with such
             officer or employee while making such inspection;

          E. Licensee or permittee has filed or encouraged another to file false information with
             the license office, the local law enforcement agency or any other agency as part
             of the license application;

          F. Licensee or permittee has contracted with two or more independent contractors
             whose business licenses were subsequently revoked in final unappealable
             decisions during a twelve-month period for engaging in unlawful conduct under the
             Utah Massage Therapy Practice Act or Prostitution under the Utah Criminal Code
             on the licensee or permittee's leased or owned premises;


          G. Any conduct or act on the business premises, or any conduct, act, or condition
             permitted by the licensee, permittee, or any employee on the business premises,
             which renders or tends to render such business or its premises: (a) a public or
             private nuisance, as defined by applicable law, regulation, or ordinance; or (b) a
             menace to the health, peace, safety, morals, or general welfare of the City or its
             residents;

          H. Licensee or permittee is responsible for unlawful activities conducted on the
             business premises that are contrary to the laws of the United States, the State of
             Utah, the ordinances of the metro, or the rules and regulations of any agency or
             another governmental entity governing the operation of the business holding the
             license or permit;

          I.   Licensee or permittee has permitted its employees, agents, or patrons to engage
               in illegal activities on the business premises or to use the premises for any unlawful
               purpose or enterprise or the maintenance of a public or private nuisance, as
               defined by applicable law, regulation, or ordinance;


                                                 6
DATE           THURSDAY                            MARCH                                 5, 2020


          J. Licensee’s or permittee’s business activities or premises are conducted or kept in
             an illegal, disorderly, unsanitary, hazardous, or ill-governed manner;

          K. The failure of the licensee or permittee to comply with any condition or requirement
             under local, state, or federal law; or

          L. Any other reason, not arbitrary or capricious and permitted by law under the
             metro’s police power that justifies suspension or revocation of a license or permit.

       Section 2: Section 5.07.030, entitled “License Suspension Or Revocation – Procedure,” is
repealed and replaced as follows:

5.07.30       License/Permit Suspension Or Revocation – Procedure

          A. If the license official has good cause to believe that a license or permit should be
             suspended or revoked, the official shall schedule a hearing before the council by
             providing notice to the licensee and permittee which notice shall contain the
             following:

              1. The time, place, and date of the hearing;

              2. A statement of the purpose of the hearing; and

              3. A reasonably definite statement setting forth the alleged grounds under Section
                 5.07.020 justifying suspension or revocation of the license or permit.

          B. The hearing shall take place at least fourteen (14) days and not more than
             thirty(30) days from the date written notice is sent.

          C. At the hearing, the licensee or permittee shall have the right: (a) to appear
             personally or by counsel; (b) to hear the evidence against him; (c) to confront and
             cross examine any witnesses; (d) to present evidence and witnesses on his behalf;
             and (e) to state his case as to why the license or permit should not be suspended
             or revoked.

          D. After conducting a hearing pursuant to this section, the council may suspend or
             revoke any license or permit if the licensee or permittee fails to show cause by a
             preponderance of the evidence why the license or permit should not be suspended
             or revoked.

          E. If the licensee or permittee fails, neglects, or refuses to appear at the hour and
             date set for such hearing, the council may proceed to determine the matter in the
             absence of the licensee or permittee, or may, in its sole discretion, continue the
             date of such hearing to some later date, and at the later date proceed to act on the
             matter without further notice to the licensee or permittee.



                                               7
DATE           THURSDAY                             MARCH                                 5, 2020

          F. After due deliberation, the council shall announce its decision, and shall issue a
             written decision notifying the licensee or permittee of its findings and determination
             and an explanation of how the licensee or permittee may appeal the council’s
             decision.

   Section 3: Section 5.07.040, entitled “Appeals,” is repealed and amended as follows:

5.07.40       Appeals

          A. A licensee or permittee may appeal any decision rendered by the council under
             this chapter by requesting an administrative hearing with the metro’s administrative
             law judge pursuant to the process set forth in chapter 1.16, which shall govern
             appeals filed under this chapter.

          B. In addition to the provisions of chapter 1.16, the administrative law judge shall:

              1. Review the record, and may not accept or consider any evidence outside the
                 record unless the evidence was offered to and was excluded by the council,
                 the

              2. license official or license official’s designee and the administrative law judge
                 determines that it was improperly excluded;

              3. Review the council’s or the license official’s actions to determine whether the
                 council’s decision was arbitrary, capricious, or illegal; and

              4. After reviewing the record and written and oral argument on both sides, affirm,
                 reverse, alter, or remand the council’s decision for further review and
                 consideration.

          C. The filing of an appeal does not automatically stay the council’s decision; however,
             the administrative law judge has the authority to stay the decision while the appeal
             is pending.

          D. A decision issued by the administrative law judge pursuant to this section is a final
             administrative order that is appealable to the district court pursuant to section
             1.15.130.

   Section 4: Section 5.07.050, entitled “Effect of Revocation,” is amended as follows:

5.07.50       Effect of Suspension or Revocation

          A. If a license or permit is suspended or revoked, it shall thereafter be unlawful for
             any person to engage in or use, or permit to be used, any property for any business
             with respect to which the license or permit has been suspended or revoked.




                                                8
DATE           THURSDAY                              MARCH                                 5, 2020

           B. In the event of suspension or revocation as provided in this chapter, there shall be
              no refund or rebate of any part of the original license or permit fee paid by the
              licensee or permittee.

       Section 5: Section 5.07.060, entitled “Effect of Revocation,” is amended as follows:

5.07.60        Waiting Period For New License Or Permit

           A. No person whose license or permit has been suspended or revoked under the
              provisions of this chapter, and no person associated or connected with such
              person in the conduct of such business, shall be granted a license or permit for the
              same purpose for a period of six months after the suspension or revocation has
              occurred.

           B. The council may, for good cause, waive the prohibition against persons formerly
              associated or connected with an individual who has had a license or permit
              suspended or revoked.

       Section 6: Section 5.02.180, entitled “License – Effect of Denial,” is amended as follows:

5.02.180 License – Effect of Denial and Waiting Period for New License

           A. If at any time a license or renewal is denied under the provisions of this chapter, it
              shall thereafter be unlawful for any person to engage in or carry on or operate or
              use or permit to be operated or used, any property for any business with respect
              to which the license or renewal has been denied, until a license or renewal shall
              be granted by the council upon appeal.

           B. No person denied a license under the provisions of this chapter, and no person
              associated or connected with such person in the conduct of such business, shall
              be granted a license for the same purpose for a period of six months after the
              denial has occurred.

        Section 7: Pursuant to Utah Code Ann. §§ 10-3-711(1)(b) & 10-3-712, this ordinance shall
take effect twenty (20) days after: (a) depositing a copy of the ordinance in the White City Metro
Township’s recorder office; and (b) publishing a short summary of the ordinance in a newspaper
of general circulation within the metro township.

        IN WITNESS WHEREOF, the Council has approved, passed, and adopted this ordinance
on this 5th day of March, 2020.

       APPROVED and ADOPTED this 5th day of March, 2020.

                                                             By: /s/ PAULINA F. FLINT

                                          RECITALS

   A. The White City Metro Township (“White City”) is a municipality pursuant to Utah Code
      Annotated (“UCA”) §§ 10-2a-401 et seq.

      for White City pursuant to UCA § 10-3b-501.

      Committee” or “Committee”) to develop the draft of the White City General Plan, pursuant

                                               11

       Planning Commission (the “Planning Commission” or “Commission”) and, if so

      and to set forth other criteria such as membership, appointments etc.

      Committee.

Resolution

               The Council reviewed the following Resolution identifying the intent to amend and
establish special land use rules, regulations, and management for FEMA-identified flood zones
located in White City to apply for inclusion in the National Flood Insurance Program;

RESOLUTION NO. 20-05-01                                                       Date: May 7, 2020

       A RESOLUTION OF THE WHITE CITY METRO TOWNSHIP COUNCIL
       IDENTIFYING THE INTENT TO AMEND AND ESTABLISH SPECIAL LAND USE
       RULES, REGULATIONS, AND MANAGEMENT FOR FEMA-IDENTIFIED FLOOD
       ZONES LOCATED IN WHITE CITY TO APPLY FOR INCLUSION IN THE
       NATIONAL FLOOD INSURANCE PROGRAM

                                          RECITALS

   A. The White City Metro Township (“White City”) is a municipality pursuant to Utah Code
      Annotated (“UCA”) §§ 10-2a-401 et seq.

   B. The White City Metro Township Council (the “Council’) is the municipal governing body
      for White City pursuant to UCA § 10-3b-501.

   C. The Federal Emergency Management Agency (“FEMA”) is an agency of the United States
      Government tasked with assisting and supporting State, County, and Local Governments
      with disaster preparation, training, incident management support, and possible emergency
      recovery efforts.

   D. Pursuant to the protection of people and property, and as part of a statewide flood zone
      identification study FEMA performed, there may be locations in White City that are
      susceptible to a threat of flooding (“Flood Zones”).

                                               3
DATE          THURSDAY                             MAY                                    7, 2020


 E. Identified flood zones include certain key facilities the public needs for the general health
    and safety of White City.

 F. Prior to the incorporation of White City on January 1, 2017, all land use decisions in flood
    zones were made and administered by Salt Lake County as the “municipal government”
    of White City.

 G. The land use ordinances and regulations regarding flood zones became White City’s
    ordinances and regulation and may need to be amended due to White City’s incorporation.

 H. Identifying, evaluating and pursuing mitigation efforts within possible flood zones, and
    recognizing flood zones in official actions relating to land use as we move forward, and
    addressing issues in the FEMA-identified flood zones in the municipality, it is the intent of
    the White City Council to ensure flood zone maps land use ordinances, regulations and
    hazard management are in compliance with FEMA regulations such that its residents can
    benefit from the National Flood Insurance Program.

 I.   Pursuant to Utah Code §§ 10-3c-103, 68-3-12.5, and 10-9a-501 et al, the Council has the
      statutory authority to adopt land use and control measures to reduce future flood losses.

                                        RESOLUTION

      THEREFORE BE IT RESOLVED, by the White City Metro Township Council,

      1. The Council assures the Federal Emergency Management Agency (FEMA)
         that it will enact as necessary, and maintain in force, in those areas having
         flood hazards, adequate land use and control measures with effective
         enforcement provisions consistent with the criteria set forth in Section 60 of the
         National Flood Insurance Program Regulations; and

      2. Vest the Flood Control Engineer with the responsibility, authority and means
         to:

         (a) Assist the Director of Planning and Development Services (the “Director’ or
         “Administrator”), at his/her request, in the delineation of the limits of the area
         having special flood hazards.

         (b) Provide such information concerning present uses and occupancy of the
         flood plan, mudslide (i.e., mudflow) or flood-related erosion areas as the
         Director may request.

         (c) Maintain for public inspection and furnish upon request, for the
         determination of applicable flood insurance risk premium rates within all areas
         having special flood hazards identified on a Flood Hazard Boundary Map
         (FHBM) or Flood Insurance Rate Map (FIRM), any certificates of floodproofing,
         and information on the elevation (in relation to mean sea level) of the level of


                                               4
DATE           THURSDAY                              MAY                                   7, 2020

           the lowest flood (including basement) of all new construction or substantially
           improve structure has been floodproofed, the elevation (in relation to mean sea
           level) to which the structure was floodproofed.

           (d) Cooperate with Federal, State and local agencies and private firms which
           undertake to study, survey, map and identify floodplain, mudslide (i.e.,
           mudflow) or flood-related erosion areas, and cooperate with neighboring
           communicates with respect to management of adjoining floodplain, mudslide,
           (i.e., mudflow) and/or flood-related erosion areas in order t prevent aggravation
           of existing hazards.

           (e) Upon occurrence, notify the Administrator in writing whenever the
           boundaries of the community have been modified by annexation or the
           community has otherwise assumed or no longer has authority to adopt and
           enforce floodplain management regulations for a particular area. In order that
           all FHBM’s and FIRM’s accurately represent the community’s boundaries,
           include within such notification a copy of the map of the community suitable for
           reproduction, clearly delineating the new corporate limits or new area for which
           the community has assumed or relinquished floodplain management
           regulatory authority.

           (f) Submit an annual report to the Administrator concerning the community’s
           participation in the program, including, but not limited to the development and
           implementation of floodplain management measures.

       3. Appoints the Greater Salt Lake Municipal Services District with the
          responsibility, authority, and means to implement the commitments as outlined
          in this Resolution.

       4. Agrees to take such other official action as may be reasonably necessary to
          carry out the objectives of the adopted floodplain management measures.

       APPROVED AND ADOPTED in the White City Metro Township, Salt Lake County,
Utah this 7th day of May, 2020.

                                             /s/ PAULINA F. FLINT
                                             Mayor
ATTEST

/s/ SHERRIE SWENSEN
Salt Lake County Clerk
Metro Township Clerk/Recorder

to a lack of a second.

year there is a mosquito abatement meeting and this year it was supposed to be held in Portland;
however, it was not held. UGT reviewed best practices and the Board got all good reviews, which
helps keep insurance costs down.

                                    −−−−−−−−−−−−−−
Greater Salt Lake Municipal Services District (GSLMSD)

              Mayor Flint stated there are some major adjustments to try and push off some
expenditures. Salt Lake County is looking at a $39 million hit. Sales tax is still collected when
purchases are made online.

                                    −−−−−−−−−−−−−−
Wasatch Front Waste and Recycling District (WFWRD)

                Mayor Flint stated the dumpster program has been cancelled in its present form.
There will be a clean-up on August 25 for a day and half. Residents will have to call in and request
a dumpster to be delivered to the property on a first come first serve basis.

                                    −−−−−−−−−−−−−−
Salt Lake County Animal Services Advisory Board

                Council Member Little stated Salt Lake County Animal Services has been closed
to the public because of COVID-19. Starting May 11, it will start to make appointments for people
to come in. There are over 90 animals that have been fostered out and 27 animals at the shelter.
The call volume has been steady and nuisance calls have been down. Complaints are coming in
for people not cleaning up after their pets at the trail heads. There have been a lot of dead animal
calls, if someone sees a dead animal, it would like a call so the animal can be scanned for a chip.
Animal Services received a grant to help pet owners who have tested positive for COVID-19.
There is a free drive-through pantry for animals at the shelter. There is training available on
Facebook for the public. For the 12th time, Salt Lake County Animal Services has received the
Best of State award.

                                    −−−−−−−−−−−−−−
Council of Governments (COG)

               Nothing to report.

                             WHITE CITY METRO TOWNSHIP


RESOLUTION NO.: ___20-06-01____________                   DATE: ____06-04-2020__________


A RESOLUTION OF THE WHITE CITY METRO TOWNSHIP COUNCIL APPROVING A
LICENCE        FOR   PIPELINE    AGREEMENT        FOR    A    NONEXCLUSIVE         WIRELESS
TELECOMMUNICATIONS INFRASTRUCTURE FRANCHISE BETWEEN TELEPORT
COMMUNICATIONS AMERICA LLC AND WHITE CITY


                                         RECITALS


A. The White City Metro Township (“White City”) is a municipality pursuant to Utah Code
   Annotated (“UCA”) §§ 10-2a-401 et seq.


B. The White City Metro Township Council (the “Council”) is the municipal legislative body
   for White City pursuant to UCA § 10-3b-501.

C. Teleport Communications America, LLC (the “COMPANY’) is a Delaware limited liability
   company that conducts business under Utah Law.

D. The Company has contacted White City to seek a nonexclusive wireless telecommunications
   infrastructure franchise license agreement (the “AGREEMENT”) from White City.

E. The agreement would allow the Company to install wireless telecommunications pipelines in
   White City to improve the efficiency of wireless telecommunications for the users of such
   services.

F. Improved wireless telecommunications services are generally desired by the residents of
   White City.

G. Entering into this agreement is to the mutual benefit of the Company and White City.
                                         RESOLUTION

       NOW THEREFORE BE IT RESOLVED, the White City Metro Township Council does
hereby approve the attached Agreement, hereinafter known as Attachment A to include its
exhibits, between Teleport Communications, LLC and White City for the work referenced in the
Agreement attached hereto, marked Attachment "A" including attachments thereto, and
authorizes the Mayor to sign the Agreement for White City.

Ordinance No.: 20-06-01                                 Date: _______________, 2020


       AN ORDINANCE OF THE WHITE CITY METRO TOWNSHIP COUNCIL
       REPEALING CHAPTER 3.52, ENTITLED "SUBDIVISION AND ZONING
       FEES," AND REPLACING IT WITH A NEW CHAPTER 3.52 THAT
       REFERENCES THE PLANNING AND DEVELOPMENT SERVICES FEE
       SCHEDULE APPROVED BY THE COUNCIL PURSUANT TO THE WHITE
       CITY MUNICIPAL CODE OF ORDINANCE 3.42.040. THE ORDINANCE
       ALSO AMENDS SECTIONS 3.48.020, 3.48.025, AND 3.48.030 OF THE
       ENGINEERING FEES CHAPTER TO LIKEWISE INCORPORATE
       PLANNING AND DEVELOPMENT SERVICES' FEE SCHEDULE.



      The White City Metro Township Council, State of Utah, ordains as follows:



      SECTION I: The amendments made herein are designated by underlining the new

substituted language. The language being deleted is designated by brackets and interlineations.

      SECTION II: Chapter 3.52 of the White City Municipal Code of Ordinances entitled

"Subdivision and Zoning Fees," is hereby repealed and replaced, as follows:

Chapter 3.52 - Subdivision and Zoning Fees

3.52.010 - Fees as set forth in fee schedule.

      Subdivision and zoning fees shall be established in accordance with Section 3.42.040 of

these ordinances. These fees shall be set out in the Planning and Development Services Fee

Schedule, which shall be approved by the Council, and shall be on file with the Planning and

Development Services Department, or Designated Agency.

      SECTION III: Section 3.48.020 is hereby amended as follows:

3.48.020-Plat filing and engineering checking fees.
                                                1
Any and all persons filing plats with the County Recorder’s Office shall first have paid all fees

required in Chapter 3.52. In addition, persons filing plats shall pay to the Planning and Development

Services Department, or designated agency, [an] the Plat Filing and [e];Engineering

[e]Checking [f]Fee set out in the Planning and Development Services Fee Schedule, which

shall be on file with the Planning and Development Services Department, or designated

Agency.[of 6.0 percent of the performance bond amount in accordance with the following

schedule:

        A.Thirty five percent of the total engineering checking fee shall be paid prior to the

        engineer review, which consists of a technical review of the subdivision plat, a field

        boundary check in accordance with policies prepared by the planning and development

        services division and the county surveyor, and construction plan approval. If the total

        engineering fee cannot be estimated prior to the engineering review, the portion of the

        total fee paid at such time shall be ninety dollars per lot, with a minimum of one

        hundred eighty dollars.



        B. The remaining portion of the engineering checking fee which, when added to the

        portion of the fee paid pursuant to subsection A of this section, will total 6.0 percent of

        the performance bond amount, shall be paid for administration of the performance bond

        and construction inspection prior to the recording of the plat or the installation of any

        proposed improvement. Construction inspection shall consist of a survey check of

        proposed improvement grades, a survey check of monuments, and inspection of

        improvement construction.]

      SECTION IV: Section 3.48.025 is hereby amended as follows:

3.48.025-Engineering checking fee for subdivisions where no plat must be recorded.

      Any person proposing to subdivide a parcel of land where no plat must be recorded (a

      nonregular subdivision) shall pay to the Planning and Development Services

      Department, or Designated Agency the [an] [e];Engineering [c]Checking [f]Fee for

      Subdivision with no plat set out in the Planning and Development Services Fee

      Schedule. which shall be approved by the Council and shall be on file with the Planning

      and Development Services Department, or Designated Agency. [in the amount of two

      hundred dollars.]

SECTION V: Section 3.48.030 is hereby amended as follows:

3.48.030-Engineering checking fees for nonsubdivision development.

      Any person proposing to develop a parcel of land, either as a permitted use[,] or

      conditional use,

but not a[n] Planned Unit Development[, other than] or a subdivision, shall pay to the

Planning and Development Services Department, or Designated Agency [an]                 the

[e]Engineering [c]Checking [f]Fee for nonsubdivision development set out in the Planning

and Development Services Fee Schedule, which shall be on file with the Planning and

Development Services Department, or Designated Agency. [in-an amount equal to 4.5

percent of the total performance bond amount for required landscaping, off site storm

drainage and on site storm drainage improvements. The minimum fee shall be one hundred

fifty dollars which must be paid prior to the engineering review. Any remaining portion of

the fee shall be paid prior to approval by the planning and development services division of

 SECTION VI: This ordinance shall take effect fifteen (15) days after its passage and upon

 at least one publication in a newspaper published in and having general circulation in Salt

 Lake County, and if not so published within fifteen (15) days then it shall take effect

                                    SUMMARY OF

           WHITE CITY METRO TOWNSHIP ORDINANCE NO.: 20-06-01

      On the    day of                       , 2020, the White City Metro Township Council
      adopted Ordinance No.: 20-06-01 repealing Chapter 3.52, entitled "Subdivision and
      Zoning Fees," and replacing it with a new Chapter 3.52 that incorporates the
      Planning and Development Services Fee Schedule approved by the County Council
      pursuant to County Ordinance 3.42.040. The ordinance also amends Sections
      3.48.020, 3.48.025, and 3.48.030 of the Engineering Fees Chapter to likewise
      incorporate Planning and Development Services' Fee Schedule.

 0-8 FY2021 UPD Tentative
Budget e-Version bookmarked
                      INTRODUCTION
The Unified Police Department of Greater Salt Lake (UPD) is an interlocal law
enforcement agency serving approximately 271,000 residents and businesses in
Greater Salt Lake. UPD is staffed by 385 SWORN officers and 100 full-time civilians. UPD
operates six precincts in the Salt Lake Valley and is headquartered in South Salt Lake
City.

Beginning July 1, 2020, UPD will provide law enforcement services and police
protection in the following communities:
   •   Brighton Township
   •   Copperton Metro Township
   •   Emigration Metro Township
   •   Holladay City
   •   Kearns Metro Township
   •   Magna Metro Township
   •   Midvale City
   •   Millcreek City
   •   Taylorsville City
   •   White City Metro Township
   •   Unincorporated areas of Salt Lake County
UPD is governed by the Board of Directors, who are appointed directly by the
communities they represent giving the agency local control. Pursuant to the Interlocal
Agreement, the Sheriff serves as the Chief Executive Officer and is the highest-ranking
officer at UPD; the Undersheriff is the Sheriff’s executive officer and serves in her stead
as needed. The Chief of Police is the operations manager of the organization, directly
supervising the Deputy Chiefs and Commanders. The Sheriff, Undersheriff, Chief of
Police, CFO, Chief Legal Counsel, and HR Director work together as the Executive
Team to monitor revenues & expenses, formulate policy, implement best practices,
minimize risk, and lead a dynamic police department.
Precincts are led by Deputy Chiefs who are selected by their respective community.
UPD’s Deputy Chiefs manage the operations at the precinct level, which includes
patrol, property crimes investigations, street level narcotics, traffic enforcement,
community-oriented policing (COP), school resource officers, domestic violence, and
victim advocacy programs.
Participating communities come together and leverage UPD’s full suite of police
services which are typically not available to smaller police departments. The UPD has
highly specialized police service teams such as Violent Crimes Investigations, Special
Victims, SWAT, K-9, Forensics, Accident Reconstruction, and Technical Services. The
structure, size and expertise of the Unified Police Department allow for operational and
financial efficiencies through economies of scale.

                 KEARNS PRECINCT




    Kearns Precinct, 4250 W 5415 S, Kearns, UT 84118                 (385)-468-9488


Salaries & benefit budget determined by HR / Finance
Operation expenditure budget prepared by Precinct Chief and approved by the Sheriff
KEARNS PRECINCT
Services Provided
About the Precinct

The Metro Township of Kearns covers roughly 4.8 square miles with a population of just over
38,000 people. Kearns has a longstanding history of being served by the Unified Police
Department, and prior to 2010, the Salt Lake County Sheriff’s Office. The Kearns Precinct is
located at 4250 West 5415 South. One Deputy Chief, one Lieutenant (Executive Officer), and
one Administrative Assistant are housed within the Kearns Precinct.

Patrol Operations

Patrol in Kearns is comprised of five Sergeants, eighteen Patrol Officers, and two full-time Traffic
Officers. To best suit the needs and safety of Kearns, the community is broken down into five
patrol districts. This allows the Patrol Officers to become more familiar with, and take ownership
of, the districts they serve.

Patrol Officers are the first responders to incidents, as they are dispatched by the Valley
Emergency Communications Center (VECC). They are also some of the most visible
representatives of the Kearns Precinct, as they pro-actively patrol the district they are
assigned. Patrol officers are provided extensive training in many different skillsets and attend
mandatory training throughout their careers to be able to handle the myriad of incidents and
situations they encounter.

Community Oriented Policing

Community Oriented Policing (COP) is a philosophy adopted by UPD, which promotes
organizational strategies that support the systematic use of partnerships and problem-solving
techniques to pro-actively address the immediate conditions that give rise to public safety
issues such as crime, social disorders, and fear of crime.

In addition to every employee adopting the COP philosophy, Kearns Precinct dedicates one
detective to community-related issues. This detective works alongside community leaders, the
Kearns Metro Township Council, and the Kearns Community Council.

Kearns has two middle schools and one high school that are serviced by three full-time School
Resource Officers (SROs). In addition to keeping our schools safe, they also enjoy teaching,
interacting, and forming positive relationships with our youth.

Investigations

The Kearns Investigations Unit and the Directed Enforcement Unit are two separate units of the
Kearns Precinct. Collectively, the units are comprised of two sergeants, one victim advocate,
and twelve detectives.

The Kearns Investigations Unit is tasked with investigating reported acts of domestic violence,
property crimes, and fraud. The Directed Enforcement Unit (DEU) conducts complex
investigations that support the safety of the Kearns community. The DEU team targets career
criminals and violent offenders, conducts high-risk arrest warrants, surveillance, and search
warrants along with intelligence collection and analysis.
KEARNS PRECINCT
In addition to precinct-level investigations, the Kearns community is served by UPD’s regional
Investigations Unit, which has over fifty specialized detectives. This specialized unit investigates
serious crimes against persons through the Violent Crimes, Cold Case Investigations, Special
Victims, Forensic Investigations, Collision Analysis and Reconstruction units, and other specialty
assignments.


Commander Budget Message
The Kearns Precinct FY 2020-2021 budget includes a request for one additional FTE, slotted for
patrol. Over the past 15-20 years, there have been added allocations approved for the
Kearns Precinct; however, during those years no additional patrol positions have been
approved. The additional FTE’s provided to Kearns in the last two years were used to meet the
needs of School and Traffic issues. With the growth that has occurred and the additional road
projects in line for Kearns Township, an additional FTE is needed to provide appropriate service
levels, which will be monitored by both “cases per officer” and “response times.”

In addition, the Kearns Precinct has been in dire need of more office space to accompany
the growth that has occurred. After negotiations with the current building owner, the Kearns
Precinct was able to obtain an extra 6,000 square feet of office space. The offer for increased
space, with additional rent obligation, was presented to the SLVLESA Board and approved.
The modifications to the lease space will be able to accommodate the Kearns Precinct for
years to come.

                MAGNA PRECINCT




    Magna Precinct, 2711 S Buccaneer Drive, Magna, UT 84044          (385)-468-9350
Salaries & benefit budget determined by HR / Finance

                 MAGNA PRECINCT
Services Provided
About the Precinct
The Magna Precinct covers roughly 7.4 miles and serves a population of nearly 30,000
residents. The Magna Precinct administration is composed of one Deputy Chief, one
Lieutenant (Executive Officer), and one Administrative Assistant.
Patrol Operations
The Patrol Division is comprised of nineteen Officers and five Sergeants. Patrol Officers
are the first responders to incidents, as they are dispatched by the Valley Emergency
Communications Center (VECC). They are also the most visible representatives of the
Magna Precinct, as they proactively patrol the Magna Community. Patrol Officers
receive extensive training in many different skillsets. They attend mandatory annual
training throughout their careers.
Community Oriented Policing
Community policing is a philosophy adopted and practiced by the Unified Police
Department. It promotes organizational strategies that support the systematic use of
community partnerships and problem-solving techniques.
Every member of the Magna Precinct embraces this philosophy and is empowered to
actively seek opportunities to facilitate proactive change. In addition, a full-time
detective is dedicated community relations and works closely with the Magna
Township and Community Councils.
Magna has two full-time School Resource Officers (SROs). These officers work with
students at the Cyprus High and Brockbank Jr. High campus. In addition to keeping our
students safe, they enjoy teaching, interacting, and forming positive relationships with
our young adults. Annually our SROs, in cooperation with other precinct detectives,
host a Youth Academy. This is a six-week course where students from the Magna
community learn about law enforcement in a positive environment.
Investigations
Two investigative units are housed within the Magna Precinct; the Magna
Investigations Unit and the Directed Enforcement Unit (DEU). The Detective Division is
comprised of nine Detectives, two Detective Sergeants, two School Resource Officers,
two Traffic Officers, and one Victim Advocate.
The Magna Investigations Unit is tasked with investigating reported acts of domestic
violence, property crimes, and fraud. The DEU conducts complex investigations that
Salaries & benefit budget determined by HR / Finance
Operation expenditure budget prepared by Precinct Chief and approved by the Sheriff
                MAGNA PRECINCT
support the safety of the Magna community. This unit targets career criminals, violent
offenders, narcotics distribution, hit and run offenses, and gang activity. The Directed
Enforcement Unit is a shared resource with the Kearns Precinct.
In addition, Magna is served by the Unified Police Department’s regional Investigations
Division, which has over fifty specialized detectives. This specialized division
investigates serious crimes against persons through several units including Violent
Crimes, Cold Case, Special Victims, Forensics, Collision Analysis and Accident
Reconstruction, SWAT, and K9.


Commander Budget Message
The 2020-2021 fiscal year budget includes a request for an additional patrol officer. This
is to accommodate the increasing population and to keep current with our planned
growth.

      Millcreek Precinct, 1580 E 3900 S #100, Millcreek, UT 84124     385-468-9620

Operation expenditure budget prepared by Precinct Chief and approved by the Sheriff
               MILLCREEK PRECINCT
Services Provided
About the Precinct

The Unified Police Department Millcreek Precinct employs one Deputy Chief, one Lieutenant,
and eight Sergeants who are tasked with supervising law enforcement operations in Millcreek
City. Our precinct presently has twenty-five patrol officers, three full-time traffic officers, seven
Street Crime Detectives and four Community Crime Suppression Unit (CCSU) Detectives, one
full time D.E.A. Metro Narcotics Detective, two Community Oriented Policing (COP)
Detectives, four School Resource Officers (SROs), one full-time victim advocate and two
secretaries.

Patrol Operations

Our team enjoys extensive law enforcement experience. Officers utilize their years of training
and knowledge from previous assignments to help solve crimes, complete traffic
investigations, and run a highly successful Community Oriented Policing program. Our
functional diversity is a tremendous benefit to the varying communities we serve.

Our operation’s mission is a top priority. Citizen surveys continually rank traffic problems,
speeding, drugs/alcohol, and crime reduction as the priorities for Millcreek. We focus intently
on the prevention of traffic accidents and congestion, while aggressively enforcing speed
compliance. This is accomplished by our committed traffic enforcement team. These
experienced officers place educational "speed trailers" near trouble zones. These trailers use
laser/ radar to remind drivers of their speeds and work as an excellent speed-reducing tool,
keeping our streets, pedestrians, and other drivers safe. The traffic enforcement team also
targets other traffic issues, such as school zone safety, red-light violations, aggressive drivers,
and speed compliance.

The Millcreek Officers engaged in pro-active patrol techniques that resulted in numerous
arrests of suspects involved in continuous criminal activities. For example, working with our
district detectives, they developed crime patterns of vehicle and residential burglars to solve
crimes and put suspects in jail. Numerous drug investigations have resulted in arrests, assisting
in removing illegal drugs and nuisance houses, as well as the ongoing homeless camps and
their populations. These important issues are mainly handled by Millcreek Street Crimes
Detectives and our Community Crime Suppression Unit (C.C.S.U.) and Patrol Officers as
needed. These units operate as a highly cohesive team!

On occasion our officers assist the Canyon Patrol units in responding to calls in nearby
canyons, which include Emigration, Parley's, Millcreek, and Big & Little Cottonwood canyons.
We pride ourselves on community service, working closely with local community councils, and
neighborhood groups to deliver the level of law enforcement service they expect and
deserve.




Salaries & benefit budget determined by HR / Finance
Operation expenditure budget prepared by Precinct Chief and approved by the Sheriff
                 MILLCREEK PRECINCT
Community Oriented Policing

Our main concept of Community Oriented Policing is "The Team.” This team involves UPD
officers and members of the community we serve.

We are very pro-active with our COP philosophy by working closely with citizens, business
owners, schools, and a myriad of other individuals and organizations. We work in conjunction
with each other to achieve our goals and objectives in reducing crime and the fear of crime.

Millcreek COP Officers provide, and get involved with, several programs for our citizens that
help to create this teamwork atmosphere. These include Neighborhood Watch, Mobile
Neighborhood Patrols, the Officer Friendly Program, which is part of The Millcreek City Promise
Program, and other crime prevention and safety programs. These committed members also
work with Millcreek’s Planning & Zoning and Code Enforcement, Adult Probation and Parole,
and a variety of other organizations.

Drugs and underage alcohol violations are among the top priorities with this unit. We actively
enforce laws pertaining to these as well as provide programs and workshops for education
and prevention. We maintain the EASY Grant (Eliminating Alcohol Sales from Youth) from the
State of Utah and participate in the "America Buckle Up" campaign. These programs are
funded by the State of Utah and provide valuable resources to assist us with our goals.

We have four full-time School Resource Officers (SROs) dedicated Skyline High School and
Wasatch, Evergreen, and Churchill Jr. High Schools. We also provide a variety of programs,
assemblies, and important services to all elementary schools in our city, the School for the Deaf
and Blind, and private schools.

Investigations

We have found that decentralizing our Property Crimes Detectives and having them assigned
at the precinct level has worked well and paid dividends. It keeps everyone in the precinct
working closely and gives more accountability and ownership of assigned areas and cases.
Moreover, the Investigators team-up with COP Officers in specific areas; because of working
together, they have solved numerous cases and been instrumental in preventing many crimes.

These Officers collaborate to work vice activity as well. With nine bars and clubs in Millcreek’s
boundaries, dealing with issues that arise, and providing enforcement can be not only
challenging but also resource intensive. However, we are pleased to report our proactive
approach and enforcement in these establishments are proceeding smoothly and efficiently.

All Investigators are housed on the second floor of the UPD Millcreek Precinct sub-station along
with the COP Detectives. Two full-time civilian support staff members are also part of this team.
We all enjoy working together as a cohesive group and are looking forward to the future, to
implement ideas and strategies, to achieve our objectives of reducing crime, reducing the
fear of crime, and to make all communities in Millcreek more safe and enjoyable.

              MILLCREEK PRECINCT
Community Crime Suppression Unit

The Millcreek Community Crime Suppression Unit (CCSU) consists of one Sergeant and four
Detectives. Millcreek has very diverse demographics, and each day, this highly visible unit
deploys together within our city. It is their mission to identify and resolve specific community
issues and criminal behaviors. Citizens and business owners are encouraged to report any
crimes or ongoing issues of concern to the Millcreek Precinct. The CCSU addresses issues
relating to narcotics, nuisance homes, warrant service, aggressively investigating and bringing
dangerous felons to justice, traffic problems, and various other issues that arise requiring
immediate response with multiple officers collectively. Examples of such situations are felonies
in progress (i.e. armed robberies); missing, lost, or endangered children, elderly or disabled.



Commander Budget Message
Millcreek Precinct provides vital services to residents as well as precinct law
enforcement officers and commanders. The FY2019-20 budget included a request for
one civilian FTE to the precinct office staff to handle an ever-increasing workload, the
additional civilian FTE allowed the precinct to continue to offer top-notch customer
service to Millcreek’s citizenry and other visitors.
UPD’s Millcreek Precinct has an excellent reputation and continually receives very high
marks from the citizens we serve…We pledge to continue to strive for the very best
customer service and cutting-edge science in policing. We will always have open
dialogues with Millcreek’s citizens and elected leaders, which we have the pleasure to
serve.
It is our honor and privilege as we continue to provide the finest in policing excellence
to those who live, work, and visit Millcreek!

  SOUTHEAST ISLANDS DISTRICT
Services Provided
The Unified Police Department partners with the many unincorporated communities located in
the southeast portion of Salt Lake County to provide all police services through the Midvale
Precinct.

The Unified Police Department (UPD) strives to make the communities it serves safer and better
places to live, work, and play. The Midvale Precinct consists of one Deputy Chief, one
Lieutenant, six Sergeants, three Property Crimes Detectives, six Shelter Resource Officers, three
COP/Directed Enforcement Detectives, two Traffic/Motor Officers and twenty-three patrol
Officers. Traffic safety has always been a concern for the citizens of Midvale, the two
dedicated traffic officers intently focus on the enforcement of all traffic laws to ensure
pedestrian and motorist safety and to help reduce traffic congestion. Our staff also includes
two full-time School Resource Officers; one is assigned to Hillcrest High School and the other is
shared between Midvale Middle School and the neighborhood elementary schools. UPD
provides D.A.R.E. (Drug Awareness & Resistance Education) training to elementary school
children to curb the drug abuse epidemic at an early age.

The Officers assigned to the Midvale Precinct have extensive law enforcement
experience. They are a highly trained and diverse group of dedicated individuals who
serve with integrity, respect, and professionalism. In addition, the Midvale Precinct is
supported by the Unified Police Department’s shared Investigations and Special Ops
Divisions. These specialized divisions investigate serious crimes against persons through
several units including Violent Crimes, Cold Case, Special Victims, Forensics, Collision
Analysis and Accident Reconstruction, SWAT, and K9.
Midvale City is a member of the Unified Police Department. As such, Midvale’s Mayor
sits on the UPD Board and has direct oversight on import issues such as the budget. This
is an excellent model for effective policing, resource sharing, and cost-effective law
enforcement that will serve citizens well into the future.

Commander Budget Message
Midvale Precinct has three distinct geographic areas of service: Midvale City, White
City Metro Township, and the Southeast Unincorporated Islands. Based on an
assessment of FTEs and case volume, precinct administration, direct supervision, and
operational costs should be allocated as follows:
      Midvale City 75%
      White City   15%
      Southeast    10%

 WHITE CITY METRO TOWNSHIP
Services Provided
The Unified Police Department (UPD) provides White City with all police services through the
Midvale precinct. The first homes in White City were built along Galena drive in 1957, with most
homes being built in the fifties & sixties. White City was granted township status by the SL
County Council in 2006. White City has a population of 5,400 residents (2010 census)

The Unified Police Department (UPD) strives to make the communities it serves safer and better
places to live, work, and play. The Midvale Precinct consists of one Deputy Chief, one
Lieutenant, six Sergeants, three Property Crimes Detectives, six Shelter Resource Officers, three
COP/Directed Enforcement Detectives, two Traffic/Motor Officers and twenty-three patrol
Officers. Traffic safety has always been a concern for the citizens of Midvale, the two
dedicated traffic officers intently focus on the enforcement of all traffic laws to ensure
pedestrian and motorist safety and to help reduce traffic congestion. Our staff also includes
two full-time School Resource Officers; one is assigned to Hillcrest High School and the other is
shared between Midvale Middle School and the neighborhood elementary schools. UPD
provides D.A.R.E. (Drug Awareness & Resistance Education) training to elementary school
children to curb the drug abuse epidemic at an early age.

The Officers assigned to the Midvale Precinct have extensive law enforcement
experience. They are a highly trained and diverse group of dedicated individuals who
serve with integrity, respect, and professionalism. In addition, the Midvale Precinct is
supported by the Unified Police Department’s shared Investigations and Special Ops
Divisions. These specialized divisions investigate serious crimes against persons through
several units including Violent Crimes, Cold Case, Special Victims, Forensics, Collision
Analysis and Accident Reconstruction, SWAT, and K9.
Midvale City is a member of the Unified Police Department. As such, Mayor Paullina
Flint sits on the UPD Board and has direct oversight on import issues such as the
budget. This is an excellent model for effective policing, resource sharing, and cost-
effective law enforcement that will serve citizens well into the future.

Commander Budget Message
Midvale Precinct has three distinct geographic areas of service: Midvale City, White
City Metro Township, and the Southeast Unincorporated Islands. Based on an
assessment of FTEs and case volume, precinct administration, direct supervision, and
operational costs should be allocated as follows:
   •   Midvale City 75%
   •   White City   15%
   •   Southeast    10%

Salaries & benefit budget determined by HR / Finance
Operation expenditure budget prepared by Precinct Chief and approved by the Sheriff
         COPPERTON TOWNSHIP
Services Provided
About the Precinct
The Copperton Township is comprised of roughly 6.5 square miles with a population of
nearly 900 residents. The Magna Precinct provides law enforcement services for the
Copperton Township. Magna Precinct Administration is composed of one Deputy
Chief, one Lieutenant (Executive Officer), and one Administrative Assistant.
Patrol Operations
Patrol services are provided in combination with the patrol operations for the
Southwest Unincorporated jurisdictions. It is comprised of four Officers, one of which is
specifically assigned to the Copperton Metro Township.
These four Officers are based out of the Magna Precinct and are supported by their
Patrol Division, which is comprised of nineteen Patrol Officers and five Patrol Sergeants.
Patrol Officers are the first responders to incidents, as they are dispatched by the
Valley Emergency Communications Center (VECC). They are also one of the most
visible representatives of the Unified Police Department for the Copperton Metro
Township. Patrol Officers are provided extensive training in many different skillsets and
they attend mandatory annual training throughout their careers.
Community Oriented Policing
Community oriented policing (COP) is a philosophy adopted and practiced by the
Unified Police Department. It promotes organizational strategies that support the
systematic use of community partnerships and problem-solving techniques.
Every member of the Magna Precinct embraces this philosophy and is empowered to
actively seek opportunities to implement proactive changes.
Investigations
Copperton does not have a dedicated detective. Rather specialized Magna Precinct
Detectives are assigned to handle investigations resulting from calls for service.
Two investigative units are housed within the Magna Precinct; Magna Investigations
and the Directed Enforcement Unit. The Detective Division is comprised of nine
Detectives, two Detective Sergeants, two School Resource Officers, two Traffic
Officers, and one Victim Advocate.
The Magna Investigations Unit investigates reported acts of domestic violence,
property crimes, and fraud. The Directed Enforcement Unit conducts complex
Salaries & benefit budget determined by HR / Finance
Operation expenditure budget prepared by Precinct Chief and approved by the Sheriff
          COPPERTON TOWNSHIP
investigations that support the safety of the Magna, Southwest Unincorporated, and
Copperton Communities. This unit targets career criminals, violent offenders, narcotics
distribution, hit and run offenses and gang activity. Our Directed Enforcement Unit is a
shared resource with the Kearns Precinct.
In addition, Copperton Metro Township is served by the Unified Police Department’s
regional Investigations Division, which has over fifty specialized detectives. This
specialized division investigates serious crimes against persons through several units
including Violent Crimes, Cold Case, Special Victims, Forensics, Collision Analysis and
Accident Reconstruction, SWAT, and K9.


Commander Budget Message
The 2020-2021FY budget forecast for the Copperton Township can be summarized in
trying to maximize funding provided. The necessity to share resources is essential for
the public safety of the citizens of Copperton. It also is essential to provide for officer
safety of the officers assigned to patrol the Copperton area.

            HOLLADAY PRECINCT




      Holiday Precinct, 4570 S 2300 E, Holladay, UT 84117     (385)-468-9920


Salaries & benefit budget determined by HR / Finance
Operation expenditure budget prepared by Precinct Chief and approved by Sheriff
             HOLLADAY PRECINCT
Services Provided
The Unified Police Department partners with the City of Holladay to provide all police
services. We take great pride in serving the residents of Holladay. The Sheriff's Office,
and now the Unified Police Department (UPD), has provided law enforcement services
to this community for more than 160 years, both as an unincorporated area and as an
incorporated city since 1999. Holladay City officials and members of the Holladay
Precinct enjoy an excellent working relationship.

The Holladay Precinct is located at Holladay City Hall. Our officers who serve the
residents of Holladay have extensive law enforcement experience. The officers
assigned to the Holladay Precinct are a highly trained and diverse group of dedicated
professionals who have a wealth of experience and expertise. They are committed to
solving crimes, improving traffic and pedestrian safety, and operating an engaged
community-oriented policing (COP) program.

In addition to precinct-level investigations, the City of Holladay is served by UPD’s
regional Investigations Unit, which has over fifty specialized detectives. This specialized
unit investigates serious crimes against persons through the Violent Crimes, Cold Case
Investigations, Special Victims, Forensic Investigations, Collision Analysis and
Reconstruction units, and other specialty assignments.

One of our priorities is to address quality of life issues that make Holladay City a great
place to live. That’s why we focus on investigating crimes that involve drugs and
alcohol, vandalism, graffiti, and burglaries. Our officers work pro-actively with residents,
business owners, and schools to help reduce the number of crimes in our area, and in
turn along the Wasatch Front. We want our residents to feel safe and know that when
a crime occurs we have the resources to resolve the issue.

UPD Holladay Precinct and the City of Holladay are partnering in providing the best,
most efficient, and cost-effective police services to our citizens.

Commander Budget Message
For the 2020-2021 budget year, our goal in the Holladay Precinct is to continue to be
fiscally conservative, while providing the highest level of law enforcement service to
the city. We recently added a new allocation of a Code Enforcement Officer and will
continue to monitor the success of that officer in that position through this new budget
year. Also, we will be adding a new motorcycle for use in traffic enforcement within
the city. We will also continue to seek additional outside funds or grants to help
further public safety in the city. We truly enjoy our partnership with the City of
Holladay.

         TAYLORSVILLE PRECINCT

Salaries & benefit budget determined by HR / Finance
Operation expenditure budget prepared by Precinct Chief and approved by Sheriff
         TAYLORSVILLE PRECINCT
Services Provided
The City of Taylorsville covers approximately 10.7 miles with a population of 62,000.
Taylorsville has a rich history that dates back to 1848, when it was settled by John
Taylor and his family. The Taylorsville Precinct is located inside Taylorsville City Hall,
which is located at 2600 W Taylorsville Boulevard.
The Taylorsville Precinct focuses on outstanding customer service, professionalism,
efficiency, and the resolution of law enforcement issues through pro-active policing.
The precinct collaborates with area neighborhood watch groups to create a
partnership between the community and law enforcement. This interaction not only
helps reduce crime, but also assists civic, school, and ecclesiastical groups in
maintaining pride in our community.
In addition, Taylorsville is served by the Unified Police Department’s regional
Investigations Division, which has over fifty specialized detectives. This specialized
division investigates serious crimes against persons through several units including
Violent Crimes, Cold Case, Special Victims, Forensics, Collision Analysis and Accident
Reconstruction, SWAT, and K9.
The Taylorsville Precinct partners with allied agencies and all UPD precincts to actively
address crimes and issues that span well beyond Taylorsville City boundaries. These
essential partnerships provide actionable intelligence that make all participating
communities safer places to live, work, and visit.


Commander Budget Message
Taylorsville Precinct’s 2020-2021 fiscal year budget does not include a request for
additional officers or detectives. Neither does the 2020-2021 budget anticipate any
major purchases of equipment. Any requests for additional staff or resources would be
made mid-year, after review of related trends and in consultation with the
administrative staff of Taylorsville City. The city is expected to grow at a nominal rate
through the addition of a large retirement community as well as some new residential
growth. It is critical that as these and other development projects materialize, and as
criminal activity adapts and changes, we stand ready to address these changes with
responsible growth in public safety.

               MIDVALE PRECINCT




   Midvale Precinct, 7912 S Main Street, Midvale, UT 84047           (385)-468-9350


Salaries & benefit budget determined by HR / Finance
Operation expenditure budget prepared by Precinct Chief and approved by Sheriff
                 MIDVALE PRECINCT
Services Provided
The Unified Police Department partners with Midvale City to provide all its police services.
Settled in 1851, Midvale is located at the center of the Salt Lake Valley and has a friendly
“small-town” atmosphere. Starting as a center for mining and industry, modern Midvale City is
home to a diverse group of over 33,000 residents of various ethnic backgrounds. Midvale
shares a spirit of friendship and community of a city “in the middle of everything.”

The Unified Police Department (UPD) strives to make the communities it serves safer and better
places to live, work, and play. The Midvale Precinct consists of one Deputy Chief, one
Lieutenant, six Sergeants, three Property Crimes Detectives, six Shelter Resource Officers, three
COP/Directed Enforcement Detectives, two Traffic/Motor Officers and twenty-three patrol
Officers. Traffic safety has always been a concern for the citizens of Midvale, the two
dedicated traffic officers intently focus on the enforcement of all traffic laws to ensure
pedestrian and motorist safety and to help reduce traffic congestion. Our staff also includes
two full-time School Resource Officers; one is assigned to Hillcrest High School and the other is
shared between Midvale Middle School and the neighborhood elementary schools. UPD
provides D.A.R.E. (Drug Awareness & Resistance Education) training to elementary school
children to curb the drug abuse epidemic at an early age.

The Officers assigned to the Midvale Precinct have extensive law enforcement
experience. They are a highly trained and diverse group of dedicated individuals who
serve with integrity, respect, and professionalism. In addition, the Midvale Precinct is
supported by the Unified Police Department’s shared Investigations and Special Ops
Divisions. These specialized divisions investigate serious crimes against persons through
several units including Violent Crimes, Cold Case, Special Victims, Forensics, Collision
Analysis and Accident Reconstruction, SWAT, and K9.
Midvale City is a member of the Unified Police Department. As such, Midvale’s Mayor
sits on the UPD Board and has direct oversight on import issues such as the budget. This
is an excellent model for effective policing, resource sharing, and cost-effective law
enforcement that will serve citizens well into the future.

Commander Budget Message
Midvale Precinct has three distinct geographic areas of service: Midvale City, White
City Metro Township, and the Southeast Unincorporated Islands. Based on an
assessment of FTEs and case volume, precinct administration, direct supervision, and
operational costs should be allocated as follows:
   •   Midvale City 75%
   •   White City   15%
   •   Southeast    10%

           TOWNSHIP DISTRICT




Salaries & benefit budget determined by HR / Finance
Operation expenditure budget prepared by Precinct Chief and approved by the Sheriff
EMIGRATION CANYON DISTRICT
The Special Operations Canyon Patrol Unit provides all aspects of law enforcement
services within the Emigration Metro Township, along with the many other canyons
located in Salt Lake County. Officers interact with the community members, conduct
pro-active patrol, manage the follow-up investigations for property and person crimes
that may occur there. Although the Township is not densely populated, its popularity
as a destination for visitors and tourists, due to its beauty and inviting atmosphere,
makes it a busy area at times.
The unique canyon environment and experience presents a need for a variety of
specialized skills above and beyond what is expected of the average patrol officer.
Canyon Officers receive education and training in the use of all-terrain vehicles,
snowmobiles avalanche transceivers, and helicopter operations to name a few.
Officers are equipped to manage and handle a wide variety of emergency and
rescue type calls. The officers work closely with the Unified Fire Authority, Salt Lake City
Division of Water Quality, Utah Department of Transportation Avalanche Forecasters,
UDOT road crews, air medical helicopters, Wasatch Powderbird Guides, Wasatch
Back-Country Rescue, U.S. Forest Service, and many others in a collaborative effort to
ensure safety. Emigration Metro Township also has an officer specifically assigned and
tasked with providing community-oriented policing (COP) duties as part of his day-to-
day assignment.
Canyon Patrol operations are supplemented during rescue operations by the Salt Lake
County Sheriff’s Office Search and Rescue volunteer team.


Commander Budget Message
For the fiscal year 2020-2021 budget, Unified Police Department continues to be
fiscally responsible to the citizens we serve. No additional operational funds have been
requested as we recognize the challenges associated with the current health and
economic needs of our community. Working together we strive to meet the desires of
the residence, and of those just passing through who want to enjoy the beauties of
nature, through collaboration and partnership.
